By the end of 2024, SiReRAG [Reference 17] emerged based on RAPTOR, offering a finer-grained definition of text recall: it measures different dimensions of needs using similarity and relevance. Similarity calculates the semantic distance between text chunks using vectors or full-text search methods, which is what RAPTOR itself does (shown on the left side of the diagram). In the first half of 2024, Microsoft published a paper on GraphRAG [Reference 8] and officially open-sourced it mid-year, quickly garnering over ten thousand stars on GitHub. What accounts for GraphRAG's popularity? This relates closely to the third pain point of RAG that we previously mentioned: the semantic gap. There are many approaches to addressing the semantic gap. In addition to improvements at the chunking stage, more can be done during the preprocessing phase. A well-known and practical solution is RAPTOR [Reference 9]. RAPTOR first pre-clusters the text content and then uses a large language model (LLM) to generate summaries of the clustered texts.\n\nOn April 1, 2024, we open-sourced the complete RAG engine, RAGFlow, which has since garnered over 26,000 stars on GitHub by the end of the year. The initial two design highlights of RAGFlow have become universal design principles for RAG architecture: First, while naive RAG systems only provided text-based chunking tools, RAGFlow introduced a semantic chunking step for unstructured data to ensure the input data quality. This involves using specially trained models to parse document layouts, avoiding the interference caused by simple text chunking tools on different data layouts. As the open-source community increasingly uses these models to parse various documents, this approach has gained widespread acceptance. Second, from the outset, we firmly adopted enterprise-level search engines to provide hybrid search as the sole back-end database. By leveraging full-text search with BM25 capabilities, we ensured precise query performance.\n\nThis year's valuable contributions focus on providing more contextual information for chunks, which proves more practical; such context provided by LLM is more reliable. By using LLMs to interpret chunk content and adding supplementary information like labels, we can partially address issues related to semantic gaps that prevent chunks from being recalled. In this year's version of RAGFlow, we have also added a step for LLMs to extract information from text chunks to improve recall performance. Whether for multimodal or textual data, the results of chunking significantly impact final outcomes. In 2025, we can expect more high-quality work in this area that will ultimately resolve issues related to data entry quality.         "text": "The first process maintains the tree structure: Retrieval begins from the root nodes and progresses hierarchically throughout the tree structure. This kind of retrieval is a bit more complicated to implement and \u201cunfriendly\u201d to multiple recalls. The second process flattens the tree structure for retrievals. It is easy to implement and integrates naturally with multiple recalls. RAGFlow implements the flattened tree structure for retrieval as proposed in the paper: After file parsing with Deepdoc, you are given the option to activate the RAPTOR switch for clustering and summary generation. The original chunks and these generated summaries are then sent to the database to establish full-text and vector indexes. Subsequent operations are similar to the traditional RAG solutions. Please note that, by default, the RAPTOR switch is off, because enabling this feature would consume more token quotas.\n\nThe idea of RAPTOR stems from a paper released earlier this year titled \u201cRecursive Abstractive Processing for Tree Organized Retrieval\u201d. As shown in the figure below, the paper presents an enhanced document processing approach: performing hierarchical clustering of document content: After dividing the original document into chunks, RAPTOR clusters these chunks in a recursive and hierarchical manner: the clustering process starts from the leaf nodes (the blue chunks in the graph), and sumarizes the leaves into higher-level information through embedding. The process is recursively executed, forming a \u201ctree\u201d structure starting from the leaves. The outcomes of the clustering process are summaries, which can be generated using LLM. The utilization of clustering and summary generation is essential because it captures finer details necessary for handling complex topic queries and multi-step reasoning in Q&A tasks.\n\nThe first process maintains the tree structure: Retrieval begins from the root nodes and progresses hierarchically throughout the tree structure. This kind of retrieval is a bit more complicated to implement and \u201cunfriendly\u201d to multiple recalls. The second process flattens the tree structure for retrievals. It is easy to implement and integrates naturally with multiple recalls. RAGFlow implements the flattened tree structure for retrieval as proposed in the paper: After file parsing with Deepdoc, you are given the option to activate the RAPTOR switch for clustering and summary generation. The original chunks and these generated summaries are then sent to the database to establish full-text and vector indexes. Subsequent operations are similar to the traditional RAG solutions. Please note that, by default, the RAPTOR switch is off, because enabling this feature would consume more token quotas.\n\nData quality: RAGFlow offers open-source data cleansing models and chunking templates to improve data quality and will continue to iterate and evolve these built-in models and tools. Data retrieval: Scenarios with clear question intents require multiple recall to retrieve the relevant context, and the current RAGFlow integrates databases with multiple recall capabilities. Challenges in retrieving answers: In many cases, searching using the question content alone does not necessarily capture the context of the answer. There is clearly a gap to bridge between the semantics of the question and answer. We can approach the final point above from many aspects, including: Implementing an external knowledge graph for query rewriting and understanding of user intentions; improving answer quality by introducing agents, enabling the LLM to better its answers through increased dialogue interactions; retrieving longer contexts for the LLM to find the answer.\n\nRAPTOR helps LLM better understand the context. This is because the upper-level nodes in the clustered tree structure possess a more \u201cmacroscopic\u201d understanding of the text, making it advantageous for scenarios requiring cross-chunk summarization or multi-hop Q&A where answers can not be retrieved directly from the corresponding context. The RAPTOR approach is one of RAGFlow\u2019s attempts to tackle the pain points in RAG\u2019s retrieval. In upcoming releases, we\u2019ll bring further enhancements.",

        "text": "We introduce the novel approach of recursively embedding, clustering, and summarizing chunks of text, constructing a tree with differing levels of summarization from the bottom up. At inference time, our RAPTOR model retrieves from this tree, integrating information across lengthy documents ...\n\nResearch on the reader component includes Fusion-in-Decoder (FiD) (Izacard & Grave, 2022), which employs both DPR and BM25 for retrieval and processes passages independently in the encoder and RETRO (Borgeaud et al., 2022; Wang et al., 2023), which utilizes cross-chunked attention and chunkwise retrieval to generate text grounded on retrieved context. End-to-end system training work includes Atlas (Izacard et al., 2022), which fine-tunes an encoder-decoder model in conjunction with the retriever; REALM (Guu et al., 2020), a bidirectional, masked LM fine-tuned for open-domain question answering; and RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) (Lewis et al., 2020), which integrates pre-trained sequence-to-sequence models with a neural retriever. Min et al. (2021) introduced Joint Passage Retrieval (JPR) model which uses a tree-decoding algorithm to handle passage diversity and relevance in multi-answer retrieval. Dense Hierarchical Retrieval (DHR) and Hybrid Hierarchical Retrieval (HHR) represe\n\nWhile this method is effective for capturing broader themes, it can miss granular details. LlamaIndex (Liu, 2022) mitigates this issue by similarly summarizing adjacent text chunks but also retaining intermediate nodes thus storing varying levels of detail, keeping granular details. However, both methods, due to their reliance on adjacency for grouping or summarizing adjacent nodes, may still overlook distant interdependencies within the text, which we can find and group with RAPTOR. Building on the idea that long texts often present subtopics and hierarchical structures (Cao & Wang, 2022; Dong et al., 2023b), RAPTOR addresses the issue of semantic depth and connection in reading by building a recursive tree structure that balances broader thematic comprehension with granular details and which allows nodes to be grouped based on semantic similarity not just order in the text.\n\nRetrieval-augmented language models can better adapt to changes in world state and incorporate long-tail knowledge. However, most existing methods retrieve only short contiguous chunks from a retrieval corpus, limiting holistic understanding of the overall document context. We introduce the novel approach of recursively embedding, clustering, and summarizing chunks of text, constructing a tree with differing levels of summarization from the bottom up. At inference time, our RAPTOR model retrieves from this tree, integrating information across lengthy documents at different levels of abstraction. Controlled experiments show that retrieval with recursive summaries offers significant improvements over traditional retrieval-augmented LMs on several tasks. On question-answering tasks that involve complex, multi-step reasoning, we show state-of-the-art results; for example, by coupling RAPTOR retrieval with the use of GPT-4, we can improve the best performance on the QuALITY benchmark by 20%\n\nFigure 1: Tree construction process: RAPTOR recursively clusters chunks of text based on their vector embeddings and generates text summaries of those clusters, constructing a tree from the bottom up. Nodes clustered together are siblings; a parent node contains the text summary of that cluster. Nevertheless, existing retrieval-augmented approaches also have flaws. The one we tackle is that most existing methods retrieve only a few short, contiguous text chunks, which limits their ability to represent and leverage large-scale discourse structure. This is particularly relevant for thematic questions that require integrating knowledge from multiple parts of a text, such as understanding an entire book, as in the NarrativeQA dataset (Ko\u010disk\u1ef3 et al., 2018). Consider the fairy tale of Cinderella, and the question \u201cHow did Cinderella reach her happy ending?\u201d. The top- ...",

In my experience implementing RAG for clients, this leads to incomplete answers. RAPTOR fixes that by starting with raw documents, embedding and clustering them, then summarizing clusters recursively to form abstraction layers. This hierarchical indexing RAG approach leverages long-context LLM integration, making it perfect for modern tools like those in LangChain. Let me walk you through the high-level intuition, drawing from my experience implementing this in real-world projects like enterprise knowledge bases. Imagine starting with raw documents as the tree's leaves. You cluster similar chunks using semantic embeddings, then summarize each cluster to distill core ideas. This process repeats recursively, forming higher abstraction levels until you have a comprehensive overview. In my work, I've found that this tree-structured approach mitigates information fragmentation\u2014something I've battled in scraping vast web corpora. It leverages long-context LLM integration, especially with post-2024 advancements like those in RAGFlow v0.6.0, making it scalable for production.\n\nRAPTOR's benchmarks demonstrate a 20% accuracy boost for high-level queries, as detailed in the arXiv paper\u2014proving its edge in real-world RAG setups. Let's walk through a real-world scenario: Applying RAPTOR to LangChain's expression language docs (about 30 documents). We loaded texts, embedded them, clustered, and summarized recursively up to three levels using models like GPT or Claude, then indexed everything in a vector store. This setup handled long-context LLM integration seamlessly, avoiding arbitrary chunk splits and scaling effortlessly with post-2024 LLM advancements like extended token limits. It creates abstraction layers, allowing retrieval of both detailed chunks and high-level summaries, mitigating information loss in large corpora. After 12 years, I've found RAPTOR's flexibility in recursion depth makes it ideal for future-proofing RAG systems with evolving LLMs.\n\nStandard kNN grabs a fixed number of chunks based on similarity, great for specifics but weak on broad synthesis. RAPTOR creates a hierarchy with recursive summarization, improving semantic coverage. In: Proceedings of the International Conference on Learning Representations (2024).\n\nRetrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems commonly use chunking strategies for retrieval, which enhance large language models (LLMs) by enabling them to access external knowledge, ensuring that the retrieved information is up-to-date and domain-specific. However, traditional methods often fail to create chunks that capture sufficient semantic meaning, as they do not account for the underlying textual structure. This paper proposes a novel framework that enhances RAG by integrating hierarchical text segmentation and clustering to generate more meaningful and semantically coherent chunks. During inference, the framework retrieves information by leveraging both segment-level and cluster-level vector representations, thereby increasing the likelihood of retrieving more precise and contextually relevant information. Evaluations on the NarrativeQA, QuALITY, and QASPER datasets indicate that the proposed method achieved improved results compared to traditional chunking techniques.\n\nUpdating these models via fine-tuning or other modifications can be challenging, particularly when dealing with large text corpora [9, 16]. One common approach to address this issue involves dividing large volumes of text into smaller, manageable chunks. This method is widely used in question-answering systems, where splitting texts into smaller units improves retrieval accuracy [4]. This retrieval-based method, known as Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) [16], enhances LLMs by allowing them to reference external knowledge, making it easier to ensure that the retrieved information is up-to-date and domain-specific. However, current retrieval-augmented methods have limitations, particularly in the chunking process. Traditional chunking approaches often fail to capture sufficient semantic meaning as they do not account for the underlying textual structure.\n\nFig. 1 illustrates our proposed RAG framework, which introduces a hierarchical segmentation and clustering pipeline to enhance chunking accuracy and retrieval relevance. MLX\\u57fa\\u7840\\n\\n**\\u5b98\\u65b9\\u4ed3\\u5e93**: https://github.com/ml-explore/mlx\\n\\n**\\u8bbe\\u8ba1\\u7406\\u5ff5**:\\n- **NumPy-like API**: \\u719f\\u6089\\u7684\\u6570\\u7ec4\\u64cd\\u4f5c\\n- **\\u60f0\\u6027\\u8ba1\\u7b97**: \\u5ef6\\u8fdf\\u6267\\u884c\\u4f18\\u5316\\n- **\\u7edf\\u4e00\\u5185\\u5b58**: \\u65e0\\u9700\\u624b\\u52a8\\u7ba1\\u7406\\u8bbe\\u5907\\u5185\\u5b58\\n- **\\u53ef\\u7ec4\\u5408\\u53d8\\u6362**: grad, vmap, jit\\n\\n**\\u6838\\u5fc3\\u7279\\u6027**:\\n```python\\nimport mlx.core as mx\\nimport mlx.nn as nn\\n\\n# \\u7c7b\\u4f3cNumPy\\u7684API\\nx = mx.array([1, 2, 3])\\ny = mx.exp(x)\\n\\n# \\u81ea\\u52a8\\u5fae\\u5206\\ndef loss_fn(params, x, y):\\n    return mx.mean((model(params, x) - y) ** 2)\\n\\ngrad_fn = mx.grad(loss_fn)\\n```\\n\\n### 3. MLX-LM\\n\\n**\\u4ed3\\u5e93**: https://github.com/ml-explore/mlx-examples/tree/main/llms\\n\\n**\\u652f\\u6301\\u6a21\\u578b**:\\n- LLaMA/LLaMA-2/LLaMA-3\\n- Mistral/Mixtral\\n- Qwen/Qwen2\\n- Phi-3\\n- Gemma\\n\\n**\\u4f7f\\u7528\\u793a\\u4f8b**:\\n```bash\\n# \\u5b89\\u88c5\\npip install mlx-lm\\n\\n# \\u751f\\u6210\\nmlx_lm.generate --model mlx-community/Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct-4bit \\\\\\n                --prompt \\\"Explain RAG in one sentence\\\"\\n\\n# \\u91cf\\u5316\\u8f6c\\u6362\\nmlx_lm.convert --hf-path Qwen/Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct \\\\\\n               --mlx-path ./qwen2.5-7b-4bit \\\\\\n               -q\\n```\\n\\n**\\u91cf\\u5316\\u9009\\u9879**:\\n| \\u91cf\\u5316 | \\u7cbe\\u5ea6\\u635f\\u5931 | \\u5185\\u5b58\\u5360\\u7528(7B) |\\n|------|----------|-------------|\\n| FP16 | \\u65e0 | ~14GB |\\n| INT8 | \\u6781\\u5c0f | ~7GB |\\n| INT4 | \\u5c0f | ~4GB |\\n\\n### 4.     "type": "tool_use",
    "name": "artifacts",
    "input": {
      "id": "compass_artifact_wf-b650f3b5-5bfb-4bec-9502-b5c667d1c7cf_text/markdown",
      "type": "text/markdown",
      "title": "Knowledge Embedding Technical Routes: A Comprehensive Landscape",
      "source": "c",
      "command": "create",
      "content": "# Knowledge Embedding Technical Routes: A Comprehensive Landscape\n\nKnowledge can be embedded into large language models through five distinct technical routes: continued pre-training, supervised fine-tuning, parameter-efficient fine-tuning, model editing, and prompt caching\u2014each offering fundamentally different tradeoffs between permanence, efficiency, and flexibility. RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) operates as an orthogonal approach that retrieves rather than embeds knowledge, making these techniques complementary rather than competing. This survey provides a systematic analysis of each route with academic references, core innovations, and comparative positioning against RAG-based approaches.\n\n---\n\n## 1. Continued pre-training adapts models to domains and tasks\n\nContinued pre-training extends a model's initial training phase on domain-specific or task-specific corpora, embedding new knowledge directly into model parameters before downstream fine-tuning.\n\n### Don't Stop Pretraining (Gururangan et al., ACL 2020)\n\nThis foundational work established the effectiveness of multi-phase adaptive pre-training for domain specialization.\n\n| Attribute | Details |\n|-----------|---------|\n| **Full Title** | Don't Stop Pretraining: Adapt Language Models to Domains and Tasks |\n| **Links** | arXiv: https://arxiv.org/abs/2004.10964 \u2022 ACL Anthology: https://aclanthology.org/2020.acl-main.740/ |\n| **Venue** | ACL 2020 (Honorable Mention for Best Paper) |\n| **Core Innovation** | Introduces **DAPT** (Domain-Adaptive Pre-training) on large domain corpora and **TAPT** (Task-Adaptive Pre-training) on unlabeled task data. TAPT achieves comparable gains to DAPT while being **60\u00d7 faster** and using **5.8 million times less storage**. |\n| **vs. RAG** | Embeds domain expertise in parameters (no retrieval overhead), but requires retraining to update and risks catastrophic forgetting |\n\n### SSR: Self-Synthesized Rehearsal (Huang et al., ACL 2024)\n\n| Attribute | Details |\n|-----------|---------|\n| **Full Title** | Mitigating Catastrophic Forgetting in Large Language Models with Self-Synthesized Rehearsal |\n| **Links** | arXiv: https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.01244 \u2022 ACL Anthology: https://aclanthology.org/2024.acl-long.77/ |\n| **Venue** | ACL 2024 |\n| **Core Innovation** | Addresses continual learning **when original training data is unavailable** (e.g., for released checkpoints like Llama-2). Uses LLM-generated synthetic instances with k-means diversity selection for rehearsal, requiring only **1% real data** for ICL demonstrations. |\n| **vs. RAG** | Internalizes knowledge via synthetic replay without external database; preferable when inference latency matters |\n\n### Catastrophic forgetting remains the central challenge\n\nThe **catastrophic forgetting** problem\u2014where learning new information degrades performance on previously learned tasks\u2014motivates most continued pre-training research. Key mitigation strategies include:\n\n| Strategy | Key Paper | Venue | Core Approach |\n|----------|-----------|-------|---------------|\n| **Elastic Weight Consolidation** | Kirkpatrick et al. | PNAS 2017 | Fisher Information Matrix identifies critical parameters; quadratic penalty prevents excessive updates |\n| **Implicit Inference View** | Kotha et al. | ICLR 2024 | Forgetting reframed as task inference drift; \"Conjugate Prompting\" recovers pretrained capabilities |\n| **Experience Replay** | Rolnick et al. | NeurIPS 2019 | Maintains replay buffer of past examples; recent work (arXiv:2508.01908) shows small replay rates outperform scaling model size |\n\nThe comprehensive survey \"Continual Learning of Large Language Models\" (ACM Computing Surveys, 2025; GitHub: https://github.com/Wang-ML-Lab/llm-continual-learning-survey) provides systematic coverage of these approaches.\n\n---\n\n## 2. Supervised fine-tuning teaches style rather than knowledge\n\nThe Superficial Alignment Hypothesis, first proposed in the **LIMA paper**, fundamentally reframes our understanding of what supervised fine-tuning accomplishes.\n\n### LIMA demonstrates quality over quantity (Zhou et al., NeurIPS 2023)\n\n| Attribute | Details |\n|-----------|---------|\n| **Full Title** | LIMA: Less Is More for Alignment |\n| **Links** | arXiv: https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.11206 \u2022 NeurIPS: https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2023/hash/ac662d74829e4407ce1d126477f4a03a-Abstract-Conference.html |\n| **Venue** | NeurIPS 2023 |\n| **Core Innovation** | Fine-tuning on just **1,000 carefully curated examples** matches models trained on 52,000+ examples or with RLHF. Proposes the **Superficial Alignment Hypothesis**: \"A model's knowledge and capabilities are learned almost entirely during pretraining; alignment teaches it which subdistribution of formats should be used.\" |\n| **vs. RAG** | SFT primarily elicits existing knowledge in preferred formats rather than adding new knowledge; RAG genuinely injects new factual information |\n\nDirect empirical validation came from **\"The Unlocking Spell on Base LLMs\"** (Lin et al., ICLR 2024; arXiv: https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.01552), which demonstrated that **92.2% of tokens** generated by aligned models are within the top-3 tokens ranked by base models\u2014shifts occur predominantly in stylistic tokens, not content.\n\n### Instruction tuning enables zero-shot generalization\n\n| Paper | Venue | Link | Core Finding |\n|-------|-------|------|--------------|\n| **FLAN** (Wei et al.) | ICLR 2022 | https://arxiv.org/abs/2109.01652 | Instruction tuning on 60+ tasks enables zero-shot generalization; FLAN-137B surpassed GPT-3 175B on 20/25 tasks |\n| **Instruction Tuning Survey** (Zhang et al.) | ACM Computing Surveys 2025 | https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.10792 | Comprehensive methodology review; GitHub: https://github.com/xiaoya-li/Instruction-Tuning-Survey |\n| **Fine-Tuning vs. RAG** (Ovadia et al.) | EMNLP 2024 | https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.05934 | RAG consistently outperforms fine-tuning for knowledge injection; LLMs struggle to learn new facts through fine-tuning |\n\n---\n\n## 3. Parameter-efficient methods reduce training costs dramatically\n\nPEFT methods modify only a small fraction of model parameters, enabling fine-tuning of billion-parameter models on consumer hardware while preserving most pre-trained knowledge.\n\n### The LoRA family dominates modern practice\n\n| Method | Year/Venue | Link | Core Innovation | Parameter Overhead |\n|--------|------------|------|-----------------|-------------------|\n| **LoRA** (Hu et al.) | ICLR 2022 | https://arxiv.org/abs/2106.09685 | Low-rank decomposition \u0394W = BA where r \u226a min(d,k); **no inference latency** (weights merge) | ~0.1% |\n| **QLoRA** (Dettmers et al.) | NeurIPS 2023 | https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.14314 | **4-bit NormalFloat** quantization + LoRA; enables **65B model on single 48GB GPU** | ~0.1% + quantization |\n| **DoRA** (Liu et al.) | ICML 2024 (Oral) | https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.09353 | Decomposes weights into **magnitude and direction**; LoRA for direction, separate magnitude vector; better matches full fine-tuning dynamics | ~0.1% |\n| **QDoRA** (Answer.AI) | Technical Report 2024 | https://github.com/AnswerDotAI/fsdp_qlora | 4-bit quantization + DoRA + FSDP; **outperforms full fine-tuning by 5%** on Orca-Math while using 9\u00d7 less memory | ~0.1% |\n\n**Adapters** (Houlsby et al., ICML 2019; arXiv: https://arxiv.org/abs/1902.00751) pioneered PEFT by inserting bottleneck layers between transformer blocks. Unlike LoRA, adapters **add inference latency** but provide stronger modularity for continual learning.\n\n### PEFT vs. RAG positioning\n\nPEFT excels at teaching models *how* to respond (style, format, domain behavior) while RAG provides *what* information to use. A 2024 study (arXiv:2409.09510) found RAG yielded **14.92% improvement** on knowledge-intensive personalization tasks versus just **1.07%** for PEFT methods\u2014confirming that RAG is more effective for pure knowledge embedding while PEFT is superior for behavioral adaptation.\n\n---\n\n## 4. Model editing enables surgical knowledge modification\n\nModel editing directly modifies specific parameters to update, correct, or delete factual associations without full retraining, based on the theoretical insight that **MLP layers function as key-value memories**.\n\n### Locating knowledge in transformers\n\nThe theoretical foundation for model editing rests on two key findings:\n\n**\"Transformer Feed-Forward Layers Are Key-Value Memories\"** (Geva et al., EMNLP 2021; arXiv: https://arxiv.org/abs/2012.14913) demonstrated that FFN layers operate as associative memories where keys (first linear layer) correlate with textual patterns and values (second linear layer) induce vocabulary distributions.\n\n**\"Knowledge Neurons in Pretrained Transformers\"** (Dai et al., ACL 2022; arXiv: https://arxiv.org/abs/2104.08696) introduced the knowledge neurons thesis\u2014specific neurons store specific facts, identifiable via integrated gradients attribution.\n\n### ROME and MEMIT enable precise factual editing\n\n| Method | Year/Venue | Link | Core Innovation | Scale |\n|--------|------------|------|-----------------|-------|\n| **ROME** (Meng et al.) | NeurIPS 2022 | https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.05262 | **Causal tracing** identifies decisive activations; **rank-one modification** writes new associations to single MLP layer | Single facts |\n| **MEMIT** (Meng et al.) | ICLR 2023 | https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.07229 | Distributes updates across **multiple critical MLP layers**; solves expanded optimization objective | **Thousands of facts** |\n| **WISE** (Wang et al.) | NeurIPS 2024 | https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.14768 | **Dual parametric memory** (frozen main + trainable side) with learned router; knowledge-sharding prevents conflicts | Lifelong editing |\n\n**EasyEdit** (Zhang et al., ACL 2024 Demo; GitHub: https://github.com/zjunlp/EasyEdit) provides a unified framework implementing ROME, MEMIT, WISE, MEND, SERAC, GRACE, and other methods across T5, GPT, LLaMA, Mistral, and Qwen architectures.\n\n### Model editing vs. RAG: fundamentally different mechanisms\n\nModel editing modifies **intrinsic parametric knowledge**\u2014edited facts become \"native\" and generalize to paraphrases. RAG augments with **extrinsic knowledge** at inference\u2014the model's internal knowledge remains unchanged. Model editing suits permanent corrections without retrieval overhead; RAG suits dynamic, traceable, easily updateable knowledge with citations.\n\n---\n\n## 5. Prompt caching optimizes repeated context processing\n\nPrompt caching stores computed key-value attention states to avoid redundant processing when the same context prefix is reused, offering up to **90% cost reduction** and **85% latency reduction**.\n\n### Three commercial implementations compared\n\n| Feature | Anthropic | OpenAI | Google Gemini |\n|---------|-----------|--------|---------------|\n| **Documentation** | docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/build-with-claude/prompt-caching | platform.openai.com/docs/guides/prompt-caching | ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/caching |\n| **Launch** | August 2024 (beta), December 2024 (GA) | October 2024 | Mid-2024 (explicit), May 2025 (implicit) |\n| **Configuration** | **Manual** (`cache_control` parameter) | **Automatic** | **Both** (implicit + explicit) |\n| **Cache Write Cost** | **+25% premium** | None | None |\n| **Cache Read Discount** | **90%** | **50%** | **75-90%** |\n| **Default TTL** | 5 minutes (1-hour option) | 5-10 minutes (24-hour option) | 1 hour (customizable) |\n| **Minimum Tokens** | 1,024-2,048 | 1,024 | 2,048-4,096 |\n| **Storage Costs** | None | None | **Per-hour billing** |\n\n**Anthropic** offers maximum control and savings (90% discount) but requires explicit configuration and charges 25% write premium. **OpenAI** provides zero-implementation overhead with automatic caching but lower discount (50%). **Google** uniquely supports very long caches with per-hour storage billing and handles large media files (video, audio, PDFs) within its 1M-token context window.\n\n### Prompt caching complements RAG architectures\n\nPrompt caching represents a **compute-memory tradeoff**\u2014storing KV states trades memory for reduced computation. In RAG pipelines, caching the retrieved context eliminates redundant processing when the same documents are queried multiple times. The relationship is synergistic: RAG retrieves relevant knowledge dynamically, while caching reduces the per-turn cost of processing that retrieved context.\n\n---\n\n## Comparative positioning across all five routes\n\n| Dimension | Continued Pre-training | SFT | PEFT | Model Editing | Prompt Caching |\n|-----------|----------------------|-----|------|---------------|----------------|\n| **Knowledge Type** | Domain patterns, linguistic expertise | Style, format, task behavior | Behavioral adaptation | Specific factual corrections | Repeated context |\n| **Update Mechanism** | Full retraining | Fine-tuning | Adapter/LoRA training | Direct parameter modification | Cache refresh |\n| **Forgetting Risk** | High | Moderate | Low | Localized | None |\n| **Inference Overhead** | None | None | None (LoRA) / Small (Adapters) | None | Reduced |\n| **vs. RAG** | Deep expertise vs. dynamic retrieval | Elicits format vs. injects facts | Adapts behavior vs. provides knowledge | Permanent edits vs. updateable DB | Complements RAG |\n\n**The fundamental insight**: These five routes modify *how the model behaves* or *what it knows parametrically*, while RAG provides *external knowledge at inference*. The optimal architecture typically combines multiple approaches\u2014PEFT for domain behavior, model editing for critical corrections, prompt caching for efficiency, and RAG for dynamic, verifiable, citable knowledge access.\n\n---\n\n## Summary reference table\n\n| Paper/System | Year | Venue | Link | Core Innovation |\n|--------------|------|-------|------|-----------------|\n| Don't Stop Pretraining | 2020 | ACL | arxiv.org/abs/2004.10964 | DAPT/TAPT multi-phase adaptation |\n| SSR | 2024 | ACL | arxiv.org/abs/2403.01244 | Synthetic rehearsal without original data |\n| LIMA | 2023 | NeurIPS | arxiv.org/abs/2305.11206 | 1K examples sufficient; Superficial Alignment Hypothesis |\n| FLAN | 2022 | ICLR | arxiv.org/abs/2109.01652 | Instruction tuning for zero-shot generalization |\n| LoRA | 2022 | ICLR | arxiv.org/abs/2106.09685 | Low-rank weight decomposition |\n| QLoRA | 2023 | NeurIPS | arxiv.org/abs/2305.14314 | 4-bit NF4 quantization + LoRA |\n| DoRA | 2024 | ICML | arxiv.org/abs/2402.09353 | Magnitude-direction decomposition |\n| Adapters | 2019 | ICML | arxiv.org/abs/1902.00751 | Bottleneck layers between blocks |\n| ROME | 2022 | NeurIPS | arxiv.org/abs/2202.05262 | Causal tracing + rank-one editing |\n| MEMIT | 2023 | ICLR | arxiv.org/abs/2210.07229 | Scalable multi-fact editing |\n| WISE | 2024 | NeurIPS | arxiv.org/abs/2405.14768 | Dual memory for lifelong editing |\n| EasyEdit | 2024 | ACL Demo | github.com/zjunlp/EasyEdit | Unified editing framework |\n| FFN as Key-Value Memory | 2021 | EMNLP | arxiv.org/abs/2012.14913 | Theoretical foundation for editing |\n| Knowledge Neurons | 2022 | ACL | arxiv.org/abs/2104.08696 | Localized factual storage |\n\nThis landscape reveals that knowledge embedding is not a single problem but a spectrum of challenges\u2014from deep domain adaptation to surgical fact correction to efficient context reuse\u2014each with its optimal technical route.",
